Over the past two years, escalating financial pressure on his household pushed him to take on more responsibility managing his family’s tight budget, coordinating his church’s food pantry logistics, and trying to secure a steady position as a community-center aide. The increased need to sit through planning meetings, handle detailed forms, and wait in lines for assistance programs clashed with his tendency to act before thinking, leading to missed benefits, errors in paperwork, and impulsive small purchases that undermined his carefully tracked spreadsheets, all of which heightened his anxiety about providing for his family. After a near eviction scare triggered by a series of impulsive financial decisions and missed appointments, his wife and parish priest both urged him to seek professional help, and he agreed when he realized his difficulty holding back and staying on track was directly jeopardizing their already fragile finances.\n- Chief Complaint: “When there’s a line or a process, I just can’t seem to wait my to go over the bills or those forms for the assistance office, I’m paying attention for a few minutes and then my mind just drifts off to the birds outside or some song in my head, and when I look back I realize I don’t even remember what line I was on and have to start over again and again.\n- S004 (Instruction_Follow through_Difficulty): My wife will ask me to call about the rent help, then stop by the church pantry to check the schedule, and by the time I’ve started the first thing I’ve already gotten sidetracked talking to someone or checking something else, and I end up coming home with only half of it done or not done at all, even though I honestly meant to finish everything.\n- S005 (Organization_Difficulty): When I try to plan out the week—what day to go to the office, what important like a deadline for turning in a form.\n- S010 (Fidgeting): Anytime I’m in a meeting at the community center or even just sitting in church, my legs are bouncing, my fingers are tapping on the table or my Bible, and I keep shifting in my seat so much that people next to me sometimes give me that look like, ‘Can you sit still?’ but I just can’t relax my body.\n- S011 (Leaving_Seat): During those long waits at the benefits office, I keep popping up from my chair to pace around, look at the posters, or go outside for a minute even though I know they could call my number while I’m gone, but sitting still in that chair the whole time feels almost impossible.\n- S012 (Feeling_Restless): Most days, especially when I’m supposed to be sitting at the kitchen table doing paperwork or helping my son with homework, I feel this buzzing inside like I’ve got to get up and move or do something else right now, and it makes me feel trapped and jumpy if I try to just sit there.\n- S016 (Blurting_Answers): In meetings about the food pantry or talking with the lady at the housing office, I find myself jumping in with answers or finishing their sentences before they’re done explaining, and then I realize I didn’t actually listen to the whole question and I’ve given the wrong information or agreed to something I didn’t fully understand.\n- S017 (Waiting_Turn_Difficulty): When I’m standing in line to talk to someone about our rent or to sign up for help, I get so impatient and antsy that I start trying to squeeze ahead, go to a different window, or leave and come back later, and that jumping around has made me lose my spot or even miss my turn more than once, which has really hurt us with the money stuff.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- onset_prior_to_12_years: Even back in elementary school, teachers were always calling my mom because I wouldn’t stay in my seat, blurted things out in class, and kept getting sent into the hallway for talking and moving around too much.\n- functional_impairment: Because I can’t sit still, wait my turn, or follow through on plans, I’ve missed appointments, lost out on benefits we qualified for, and blown parts of our tight budget, which has put my family at real risk of falling behind on rent and bills.\n- duration: These problems with getting restless, acting before I think, and not being able to stick with tasks Over the past two years, escalating financial pressure on his household pushed him to take on more responsibility managing his family’s tight budget, coordinating his church’s food pantry logistics, and trying to secure a steady position as a community-center aide. The increased need to sit through planning meetings, handle detailed forms, and wait in lines for assistance programs clashed with his tendency to act before thinking, leading to missed benefits, errors in paperwork, and impulsive small purchases that undermined his carefully tracked spreadsheets, all of which heightened his anxiety about providing for his family. After a near eviction scare triggered by a series of impulsive financial decisions and missed appointments, his wife and parish priest both urged him to seek professional help, and he agreed when he realized his difficulty holding back and staying on track was directly jeopardizing their already fragile finances.\n- Chief Complaint: “When there’s a line or a process, I just can’t seem to wait my helping my son with homework, I feel this buzzing inside like I’ve got to get up and move or do something else right now, and it makes me feel trapped and jumpy if I try to just sit there.\n- S016 stop by the church pantry to check the schedule, and by the time I’ve started the first thing I’ve already gotten sidetracked talking to someone or checking something else, and I end up coming home with only half of it done or not done at all, even though I honestly meant to finish everything.\n- S005 (Organization_Difficulty): When I try to plan out the week—what day to go to the office, what day to help at church, what day to look for work—I just stare at the paper feeling lost, jumping from one idea to another, and I usually end up doing things last minute or forgetting something important like a deadline for turning in a form.\n- S010 (Fidgeting): Anytime I’m in a meeting at the community center or even just sitting in church, my legs are bouncing, my fingers are tapping on the table or my Bible, and I keep shifting in my seat so much that people next to me sometimes give me that look like, ‘Can you sit still?’ but I just can’t relax my body.\n- S011 (Leaving_Seat): During those long waits at the benefits office, I keep popping up from my chair to pace around, look at the posters, or go outside for a minute even though I know they could call my number while I’m gone, but sitting still in that chair the whole time feels almost impossible.\n- S012 (Feeling_Restless): Most days, especially when I’m supposed to be sitting at the kitchen table doing paperwork or helping my son with homework, I feel this buzzing inside like I’ve got to get up and move or do something else right now, and it makes me feel trapped and jumpy if I try to just sit there.\n- S016 (Blurting_Answers): In meetings about the food pantry or talking with the lady at the housing office, I find myself jumping in with answers or finishing their sentences before they’re done explaining, and then I realize I didn’t actually listen to the whole question and I’ve given the wrong information or agreed to something I didn’t fully understand.\n- S017 (Waiting_Turn_Difficulty): When I’m standing in line to talk to someone about our rent or to sign up for help, I get so impatient and antsy that I start trying to squeeze ahead, go to a different window, or leave and come back later, and that jumping around has made me lose my spot or even miss my turn more than once, which has really hurt us with the money stuff.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- onset_prior_to_12_years: Or is it mainly this impatience with waiting and lines?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely goes beyond just waiting in lines. I constantly find myself interrupting people, blurting out answers or finishing their sentences during meetings and appointments before they're even done speaking. I also make impulsive little purchases that completely ruin our budget spreadsheets and cause us a lot of financial strain.\n\nDoctor: Did these impulsive and interrupting behaviors start in childhood or early adolescence, and have they been present in more than one setting (such as home, school, and work)?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely started way back in elementary school; my teachers were always calling my mom because I couldn't stay in my seat, blurted things out, and kept getting sent to the hallway. It has always been an issue in different places, from school back then to now at home with my family, at church, and at the community center.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble paying attention to details, staying focused, or finishing tasks because your mind wanders or you get distracted easily?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really struggle with staying focused and finishing tasks. When I try to go over bills or paperwork, my mind quickly drifts off to a song in my head or the birds outside, and I have to keep starting over. I also get easily sidetracked by conversations or other things when I'm trying to get things done, so I often end up leaving tasks half-finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have clear symptoms of hyperactivity, such as feeling restless, unable to stay seated, fidgeting a lot, or needing to move around constantly?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. I constantly have this \"buzzing\" feeling inside that makes me feel restless, and my legs are always bouncing or my fingers tapping during church or meetings. It gets so bad that I can't even stay in my seat during long waits at the benefits office and just have to get up and pace around.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose your temper, argue with people, deliberately refuse to do what they ask, or feel resentful or spiteful toward them?\n\nPatient: No, not at all, I don't really lose my temper or argue with people, and I never refuse to do things out of spite. If I don't follow through on what my wife or others ask, it's strictly because I get sidetracked or impatient, not because I'm trying to be defiant or resentful."

and then I realize I didn’t actually listen to the whole question and I’ve given the wrong information or agreed to something I didn’t fully understand.\n- S017 (Waiting_Turn_Difficulty): When I’m standing in line to talk to someone about our rent or to sign up for help, I get so impatient and antsy that I start trying to squeeze ahead, go to a different window, or leave and come back later,     "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in today. What brings you in?\n\nPatient: When there’s a line or a process, I just can’t seem to wait my turn without jumping ahead or changing plans, and it keeps messing up our money and appointments. It's gotten to the point where it's putting my family's finances in a really bad spot, and I knew I had to get some help.\n\nDoctor: When you jump ahead or change plans, is it mainly because you feel driven to do it impulsively, or because you are deliberately arguing, defying rules, or refusing requests from others?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's definitely not because I want to break rules or argue with anyone. It's just this really antsy, impatient feeling where I act before I even think, and I just can't seem to hold myself back. I'm not trying to be difficult, my body just moves before my brain has a chance to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you also act impulsively in other areas, like spending money, interrupting others, making quick decisions, or doing risky things without thinking first? Or is it mainly this impatience with waiting and lines?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely goes beyond just waiting in lines. I constantly find myself interrupting people, blurting out answers or finishing their sentences during meetings and appointments before they're even done speaking. I also make impulsive little purchases that completely ruin our budget spreadsheets and cause us a lot of financial strain.\n\nDoctor: Did these impulsive and interrupting behaviors start in childhood or early adolescence, and have they been present in more than one setting (such as home, school, and work)?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely started way back in elementary school; my teachers were always calling my mom because I couldn't stay in my seat, blurted things out, and kept getting sent to the hallway. It has always been an issue in different places, from school back then to now at home with my family, at church, and at the community center.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble paying attention to details, staying focused, or finishing tasks because your mind wanders or you get distracted easily?\n\nPatient: Yes, I really struggle with staying focused and finishing tasks. When I try to go over bills or paperwork, my mind quickly drifts off to a song in my head or the birds outside, and I have to keep starting over. I also get easily sidetracked by conversations or other things when I'm trying to get things done, so I often end up leaving tasks half-finished.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have clear symptoms of hyperactivity, such as feeling restless, unable to stay seated, fidgeting a lot, or needing to move around constantly?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. I constantly have this \"buzzing\" feeling inside that makes me feel restless, and my legs are always bouncing or my fingers tapping during church or meetings. It gets so bad that I can't even stay in my seat during long waits at the benefits office and just have to get up and pace around.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose your temper, argue with people, deliberately refuse to do what they ask, or feel resentful or spiteful toward them?\n\nPatient: No, not at all, I don't really lose my temper or argue with people, and I never refuse to do things out of spite. If I don't follow through on what my wife or others ask, it's strictly because I get sidetracked or impatient, not because I'm trying to be defiant or resentful.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have periods of needing much less sleep than usual and still feeling unusually energized or overly confident, or is your sleep generally normal?\n\nPatient: I don't really have times where I need way less sleep or feel overly confident, and my sleep is generally normal. Sometimes it can just be hard to wind down at night because my body is still feeling so restless and full of energy. Other than that, I don't experience those kinds of highs or bursts of extra energy.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did these attention, impulsivity, and restlessness symptoms cause problems at school or at home, and are they still clearly causing problems in your daily life now?"

It has gotten so bad that we are now at real risk of falling behind on our rent.
